Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Two bears are playing tic-tac-toe via mail. It's boring for them to play usual tic-tac-toe game, so they are a playing modified version of this game. Here are its rules.

The game is played on the following field.

<image>

Players are making moves by turns. At first move a player can put his chip in any cell of any small field. For following moves, there are some restrictions: if during last move the opposite player put his chip to cell with coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move should be done in one of the cells of the small field with coordinates (xl, yl). For example, if in the first move a player puts his chip to lower left cell of central field, then the second player on his next move should put his chip into some cell of lower left field (pay attention to the first test case). If there are no free cells in the required field, the player can put his chip to any empty cell on any field.

You are given current state of the game and coordinates of cell in which the last move was done. You should find all cells in which the current player can put his chip.

A hare works as a postman in the forest, he likes to foul bears. Sometimes he changes the game field a bit, so the current state of the game could be unreachable. However, after his changes the cell where the last move was done is not empty. You don't need to find if the state is unreachable or not, just output possible next moves according to the rules.

Input

First 11 lines contains descriptions of table with 9 rows and 9 columns which are divided into 9 small fields by spaces and empty lines. Each small field is described by 9 characters without spaces and empty lines. character "x" (ASCII-code 120) means that the cell is occupied with chip of the first player, character "o" (ASCII-code 111) denotes a field occupied with chip of the second player, character "." (ASCII-code 46) describes empty cell.

The line after the table contains two integers x and y (1 ≤ x, y ≤ 9). They describe coordinates of the cell in table where the last move was done. Rows in the table are numbered from up to down and columns are numbered from left to right.

It's guaranteed that cell where the last move was done is filled with "x" or "o". Also, it's guaranteed that there is at least one empty cell. It's not guaranteed that current state of game is reachable.

Output

Output the field in same format with characters "!" (ASCII-code 33) on positions where the current player can put his chip. All other cells should not be modified.

Examples

Input

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... x.. ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...
6 4


Output

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... ... ... 

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... x.. ... 

!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 



Input

xoo x.. x..
ooo ... ...
ooo ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...
7 4


Output

xoo x!! x!! 
ooo !!! !!! 
ooo !!! !!! 

x!! x!! x!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

x!! x!! x!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 



Input

o.. ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... xxx ...
... xox ...
... ooo ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...
5 5


Output

o!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

!!! xxx !!! 
!!! xox !!! 
!!! ooo !!! 

!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

Note

In the first test case the first player made a move to lower left cell of central field, so the second player can put a chip only to cells of lower left field.

In the second test case the last move was done to upper left cell of lower central field, however all cells in upper left field are occupied, so the second player can put his chip to any empty cell.

In the third test case the last move was done to central cell of central field, so current player can put his chip to any cell of central field, which is already occupied, so he can move anywhere. Pay attention that this state of the game is unreachable.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9x9 grid divided into 9 smaller 3x3 fields
2. Players take turns placing chips (x or o)
3. The rule: after a player places a chip at position (xl, yl) in some small field, the next player must place their chip in one of the cells of the small field at coordinates (xl, yl)
4. If all cells in that required field are occupied, the next player can place in any empty cell
5. We need to find all valid next moves for the current player

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 9 lines of the 9x9 grid (with spaces between fields)
- Read the coordinates (x, y) of the last move

### Step 2: Determine the field and position
- The grid is divided into 9 fields: (1,1) to (3,3) where each field is 3x3
- Position (x, y) maps to field: (x//3, y//3) and position within field: (x%3, y%3)
- However, we need to find which small field was just modified

### Step 3: Apply the rule
- If the last move was at (x, y), then the current player must play in the small field at (x//3, y//3)
- If that field has no empty cells, the current player can play anywhere
- Otherwise, the current player can only play in empty cells of that specific field

### Step 4: Generate output
- Convert the original grid to output
- Mark valid positions with "!" instead of "."

## Implementation

```python
# Read the 9 lines of the grid
grid = []
for _ in range(9):
    line = input().strip()
    grid.append(line)

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Determine which small field was just modified
field_x = x // 3
field_y = y // 3

# Determine the position within that field
pos_x = x % 3
pos_y = y % 3

# Create result grid
result = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        # Check if this position is in the same field as the last move
        current_field_x = i // 3
        current_field_y = j // 3
        
        # If we are in the field that was just modified, check if it's empty
        if current_field_x == field_x and current_field_y == field_y:
            # This cell is within the restricted field
            # Check if it's empty in the original grid
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                row.append('!')
            else:
                row.append(grid[i][j])
        else:
            # We are in a different field - we can play here if it's empty
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                row.append('!')
            else:
                row.append(grid[i][j])
    
    result.append(''.join(row))

# Output the result
for row in result:
    print(row)
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9x9 tic-tac-toe grid divided into 9 smaller 3x3 fields (positions 1-9 in a 3x3 grid)
2. Players take turns, with special rules about where they can place chips
3. After the first move, if a player places a chip at position (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move must be in the small field at position (xl, yl)
4. If there are no free cells in that required field, the player can place in any empty cell
5. We need to find all possible next moves for the current player

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 9 lines of the 9x9 grid
- Parse the grid into 9 separate 3x3 fields
- Read the coordinates (x, y) where the last move was made

### Step 2: Determine which field the last move was in
- Convert 1-9 coordinates to 0-8 indices
- Find which of the 9 small fields contains the position (x-1, y-1)

### Step 3: Determine possible moves
- If the field at (xl, yl) has free cells, the current player can only move in that field
- If the field at (xl, yl) has no free cells, the current player can move in any empty cell

### Step 4: Mark the output
- For each empty cell in the correct field or any field (if no free cells), mark with "!"
- Keep the original characters for occupied cells

### Step 5: Output the result

```python
# Read the 9 lines of the grid
grid = []
for _ in range(9):
    line = input().strip()
    grid.append(line)

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Find which small field the last move was in
# The small fields are arranged in 3x3 grid
# Position (x, y) belongs to field (x//3, y//3)
field_x = x // 3
field_y = y // 3

# Find the 3x3 subgrid that contains the last move
# The subgrid at (field_x, field_y) has rows [field_x*3 : (field_x+1)*3] and cols [field_y*3 : (field_y+1)*3]
start_row = field_x * 3
end_row = (field_x + 1) * 3
start_col = field_y * 3
end_col = (field_y + 1) * 3

# Create the result grid
result = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        # Determine which small field this cell belongs to
        cell_field_x = i // 3
        cell_field_y = j // 3
        
        # Check if this cell is in the required field
        if cell_field_x == field_x and cell_field_y == field_y:
            # This cell is in the field where the last move was made
            # Check if it's empty (still has ".")
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                row.append('!')
            else:
                row.append(grid[i][j])
        else:
            # This cell is not in the required field
            # It can only be played if there are no free cells in the required field
            # First, check if the required field has any empty cells
            required_has_empty = False
            for r in range(start_row, end_row):
                for c in range(start_col, end_col):
                    if grid[r][c] == '.':
                        required_has_empty = True
                        break
                if required_has_empty:
                    break
                    
            if required_has_empty:
                # Required field has free cells, so we can only play in the required field
                row.append(grid[i][j])
            else:
                # Required field has no free cells, so we can play anywhere
                if grid[i][j] == '.':
                    row.append('!')
                else:
                    row.append(grid[i][j])
                    
    result.append(''.join(row))

# Output the result
for row in result:
    print(row)
```

In example 1: Last move at (6,4) which is row 5, col 3 (0-indexed). This belongs to field (5//3=1, 3//3=1) = central field (1,1).

So the current player must play in field (1,1), which is the central 3x3 field at rows 3-5, cols 3-5.

Looking at the input, the central field has only the lower left cell (row 5, col 3) occupied with "x", so there are free cells.

The output shows that all cells in the lower left field (field (1,1)) are marked with "!" in the output.

Actually, let me re-read the problem. The last move was to "lower left cell of central field" which is position (6,4) - this is field (1,1) in 0-indexed (rows 3-5, cols 3-5).

The rule says: if last move was at (xl, yl), next move should be in field (xl, yl).

So I need to find which field contains the last move position.

```python
# Read the 9 lines of the grid
grid = []
for _ in range(9):
    line = input().strip()
    grid.append(line)

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Find which small field the last move was in
# The small fields are arranged in 3x3 grid
# Position (x, y) belongs to field (x//3, y//3)
field_x = x // 3
field_y = y // 3

# Find the 3x3 subgrid that contains the last move
# The subgrid at (field_x, field_y) has rows [field_x*3 : (field_x+1)*3] and cols [field_y*3 : (field_y+1)*3]
start_row = field_x * 3
end_row = (field_x + 1) * 3
start_col = field_y * 3
end_col = (field_y + 1) * 3

# Create the result grid
result = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        # Determine which small field this cell belongs to
        cell_field_x = i // 3
        cell_field_y = j // 3
        
        # Check if the required field has any empty cells
        required_has_empty = False
        for r in range(start_row, end_row):
            for c in range(start_col, end_col):
                if grid[r][c] == '.':
                    required_has_empty = True
                    break
            if required_has_empty:
                break
                
        if cell_field_x == field_x and cell_field_y == field_y:
            # This cell is in the field where the last move was made
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                row.append('!')
            else:
                row.append(grid[i][j])
        else:
            # This cell is not in the required field
            if required_has_empty:
                # Required field has free cells, so can only play in the required field
                row.append(grid[i][j])
            else:
                # Required field has no free cells, so can play anywhere
                if grid[i][j] == '.':
                    row.append('!')
                else:
                    row.append(grid[i][j])
                    
    result.append(''.join(row))

# Output the result
for row in result:
    print(row)

For following moves, there are some restrictions: if during last move the opposite player put his chip to cell with coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move should be done in one of the cells of the small field with coordinates (xl, yl). For example, if in the first move a player puts his chip to lower left cell of central field, then the second player on his next move should put his chip into some cell of lower left field (pay attention to the first test case). If there are no free cells in the required field, the player can put his chip to any empty cell on any field.

You are given current state of the game and coordinates of cell in which the last move was done. You should find all cells in which the current player can put his chip.

A hare works as a postman in the forest, he likes to foul bears. Sometimes he changes the game field a bit, so the current state of the game could be unreachable. However, after his changes the cell where the last move was done is not empty. You don't need to find if the state is unreachable or not, just output possible next moves according to the rules.

Input

First 11 lines contains descriptions of table with 9 rows and 9 columns which are divided into 9 small fields by spaces and empty lines. Each small field is described by 9 characters without spaces and empty lines. character "x" (ASCII-code 120) means that the cell is occupied with chip of the first player, character "o" (ASCII-code 111) denotes a field occupied with chip of the second player, character "." (ASCII-code 46) describes empty cell.

The line after the table contains two integers x and y (1 ≤ x, y ≤ 9). They describe coordinates of the cell in table where the last move was done. Rows in the table are numbered from up to down and columns are numbered from left to right.

It's guaranteed that cell where the last move was done is filled with "x" or "o". Also, it's guaranteed that there is at least one empty cell. It's not guaranteed that current state of game is reachable.

Output

Output the field in same format with characters "!" (ASCII-code 33) on positions where the current player can put his chip. All other cells should not be modified.

In the second test case the last move was done to upper left cell of lower central field, however all cells in upper left field are occupied, so the second player can put his chip to any empty cell.

In the third test case the last move was done to central cell of central field, so current player can put his chip to any cell of central field, which is already occupied, so he can move anywhere. Pay attention that this state of the game is unreachable.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9×9 grid divided into 9 smaller 3×3 fields
2. The game has special rules for moves:
   - First move: player can put chip in any cell
   - Subsequent moves: if the last move was at coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move must be in the small field at coordinates (xl, yl)
   - If that field has no empty cells, the player can move to any empty cell

3. We need to find all possible next moves for the current player given:
   - Current state of the game (9×9 grid)
   - Coordinates of the last move (x, y)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 11 lines of the grid (9 lines of 3 characters each, with spaces between fields)
- Parse the last move coordinates (x, y)

### Step 2: Determine which small field the last move was in
- The grid is divided into 9 fields: positions (1-3, 1-3) representing row groups and column groups
- For coordinates (x, y), the field index is: (x-1)//3, (y-1)//3
- This gives us which 3×3 field the last move was in

### Step 3: Determine valid moves
- If the field where the last move was made has empty cells, the next move must be in that same field
- If the field has no empty cells, the next move can be anywhere
- Mark all empty cells in the target field with "!" (but only if they're empty)

### Step 4: Output the result
- Print the grid with "!" in valid positions, and keep existing "x", "o" as is

## Implementation

```python
# Read the 9 rows of the grid (each row has 3 fields separated by spaces)
grid = []
for _ in range(9):
    row = input().strip()
    grid.append(row)

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Find which small field the last move was in
field_x = x // 3  # 0, 1, or 2 (row group)
field_y = y // 3  # 0, 1, or 2 (column group)

# Determine the target field for next move
# The next move must be in the field at (field_x, field_y)
target_field_x = field_x
target_field_y = field_y

# Create a new grid to store the result
result = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        row.append(grid[i][j])
    result.append(row)

# Check if there are any empty cells in the target field
has_empty_in_target = False
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row_idx = target_field_x * 3 + i
        col_idx = target_field_y * 3 + j
        if result[row_idx][col_idx] == '.':
            has_empty_in_target = True
            break
    if has_empty_in_target:
        break

# If there are empty cells in the target field, mark all empty cells in that field with "!"
if has_empty_in_target:
    for i in range(3):
        for j in range(3):
            row_idx = target_field_x * 3 + i
            col_idx = target_field_y * 3 + j
            if result[row_idx][col_idx] == '.':
                result[row_idx][col_idx] = '!'
else:
    # If no empty cells in target field, the player can move anywhere
    # So mark all empty cells with "!"
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if result[i][j] == '.':
                result[i][j] = '!'

# Output the result
for i in range(9):
    print(' '.join(result[i]))
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9x9 grid divided into 9 smaller 3x3 fields (positions 1-9 from top-left to bottom-right)
2. Players take turns making moves
3. The first move can be anywhere
4. After the first move, if the opponent plays at coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next player must play in the small field at position (xl, yl) (same row/col of the grid)
5. If there are no empty cells in the required small field, the player can play anywhere
6. We need to find all possible next moves for the current player

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 11 lines of the 9x9 grid (with spaces and empty lines separating the 9 fields)
- Parse the coordinates of the last move (x,y)

### Step 2: Map coordinates to small fields
- Coordinates (1-9, 1-9) map to small fields:
  - Field row: (x-1)//3 (0-2)
  - Field col: (y-1)//3 (0-2)
- So the last move was in field at (x-1)//3, (y-1)//3

### Step 3: Determine valid moves
- Find which small field was just played in (based on the last move)
- If that small field has no empty cells, the current player can play in any empty cell
- If that small field has empty cells, the current player must play in that specific small field

### Step 4: Output the result
- Copy the original grid
- Mark with "!" in all valid positions (where the current player can play)
- Keep original "x", "o", and "." in other positions

### Step 5: Implementation

# Process each of the 9 rows

    # Each row has 3 fields
    row_index = i // 3
    field_row_idx = i % 3

for i in range(9):
    line = grid_lines[i]
    # Split by space
    parts = line.split()
    # Each part is a 3-character string
    for j in range(3):
        if j < len(parts):
            field = parts[j]
            for k in range(3):
                complete_grid[i][j*3 + k] = field[k]

# Now find the last move coordinates

# Find which small field the last move was in

for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row = field_x * 3 + i
        col = field_y * 3 + j
        if complete_grid[row][col] == '.':
            has_empty_in_target = True
            break
    if has_empty_in_target:
        break

# If the target field has no empty cells, we can play anywhere
# Otherwise, we can only play in the target field (where the last move was)

output_grid = [['.' for _ in range(9)] for _ in range(9)]

for i in range(9):
    for j in range(9):
        output_grid[i][j] = complete_grid[i][j]

# Mark possible moves with "!" 
if has_empty_in_target:
    # Only possible moves are in the target field
    for i in range(3):
        for j in range(3):
            row = field_x * 3 + i
            col = field_y * 3 + j
            if complete_grid[row][col] == '.':
                output_grid[row][col] = '!'
else:
    # Any empty cell is valid
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if complete_grid[i][j] == '.':
                output_grid[i][j] = '!'
                
# Output the result in the same format
for i in range(9):
    if i < 3:
        print(' '.join(output_grid[i][0:3]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(output_grid[i][3:6]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(output_grid[i][6:9]))
    elif i < 6:
        print(' '.join(output_grid[i][0:3]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(output_grid[i][3:6]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(output_grid[i][6:9]))
    else:
        print(' '.join(output_grid[i][0:3]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(output_grid[i][3:6]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(output_grid[i][6:9]))

    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                result[i][j] = '!'

# Output in the required format
for i in range(9):
    if i < 3:
        print(' '.join(result[i][0:3]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(result[i][3:6]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(result[i][6:9]))
    elif i < 6:
        print(' '.join(result[i][0:3]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(result[i][3:6]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(result[i][6:9]))
    else:
        print(' '.join(result[i][0:3]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(result[i][3:6]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(result[i][6:9]))
```

View Extracted Code 
# Read all 11 lines
lines = []
for _ in range(11):
    lines.append(input().strip())

# Read the coordinates of the last move
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Create the complete 9x9 grid
grid = [['.' for _ in range(9)] for _ in range(9)]

# Parse the input lines to fill the grid
for i in range(9):
    line = lines[i]
    parts = line.split()
    for j in range(3):
        if j < len(parts):
            field = parts[j]
            for k in range(3):
                grid[i][j*3 + k] = field[k]

# Find which small field the last move was in
field_x = x // 3
field_y = y // 3

# Determine if there are any empty cells in the target field
target_has_empty = False
for r in range(3):
    for c in range(3):
        row = field_x * 3 + r
        col = field_y * 3 + c
        if grid[row][col] == '.':
            target_has_empty = True
            break
    if target_has_empty:
        break

# Create output grid
result = [['.' for _ in range(9)] for _ in range(9)]

# Copy original values
for i in range(9):
    for j in range(9):
        result[i][j] = grid[i][j]

# Mark possible moves
if target_has_empty:
    # Only in the target field
    for r in range(3):
        for c in range(3):
            row = field_x * 3 + r
            col = field_y * 3 + c
            if grid[row][col] == '.':
                result[row][col] = '!'
else:
    # Any empty cell
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                result[i][j] = '!'

# Output in the required format
for i in range(9):
    if i < 3:
        print(' '.join(result[i][0:3]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(result[i][3:6]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(result[i][6:9]))
    elif i < 6:
        print(' '.join(result[i][0:3]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(result[i][3:6]) + ' ' +

    else:
        print(' '.join(result[i][0:3]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(result[i][3:6]) + ' ' + 
              ' '.join(result[i][6:9]))

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Two bears are playing tic-tac-toe via mail. It's boring for them to play usual tic-tac-toe game, so they are a playing modified version of this game. Here are its rules.

The game is played on the following field.

<image>

Players are making moves by turns. At first move a player can put his chip in any cell of any small field. For following moves, there are some restrictions: if during last move the opposite player put his chip to cell with coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move should be done in one of the cells of the small field with coordinates (xl, yl). For example, if in the first move a player puts his chip to lower left cell of central field, then the second player on his next move should put his chip into some cell of lower left field (pay attention to the first test case). If there are no free cells in the required field, the player can put his chip to any empty cell on any field.

You are given current state of the game and coordinates of cell in which the last move was done. You should find all cells in which the current player can put his chip.

A hare works as a postman in the forest, he likes to foul bears. Sometimes he changes the game field a bit, so the current state of the game could be unreachable. However, after his changes the cell where the last move was done is not empty. You don't need to find if the state is unreachable or not, just output possible next moves according to the rules.

Input

First 11 lines contains descriptions of table with 9 rows and 9 columns which are divided into 9 small fields by spaces and empty lines. Each small field is described by 9 characters without spaces and empty lines. character "x" (ASCII-code 120) means that the cell is occupied with chip of the first player, character "o" (ASCII-code 111) denotes a field occupied with chip of the second player, character "." (ASCII-code 46) describes empty cell.

The line after the table contains two integers x and y (1 ≤ x, y ≤ 9). They describe coordinates of the cell in table where the last move was done. Rows in the table are numbered from up to down and columns are numbered from left to right.

It's guaranteed that cell where the last move was done is filled with "x" or "o". Also, it's guaranteed that there is at least one empty cell. It's not guaranteed that current state of game is reachable.

Output

Output the field in same format with characters "!" (ASCII-code 33) on positions where the current player can put his chip. All other cells should not be modified.

Examples

Input

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... x.. ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...
6 4


Output

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... ... ... 

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... x.. ... 

!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 



Input

xoo x.. x..
ooo ... ...
ooo ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...
7 4


Output

xoo x!! x!! 
ooo !!! !!! 
ooo !!! !!! 

x!! x!! x!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

x!! x!! x!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!!

o.. ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... xox ...
... ooo ...

... ... ...
... ... ...

!!! xox !!!

Note

In the first test case the first player made a move to lower left cell of central field, so the second player can put a chip only to cells of lower left field.

In the second test case the last move was done to upper left cell of lower central field, however all cells in upper left field are occupied, so the second player can put his chip to any empty cell.

In the third test case the last move was done to central cell of central field, so current player can put his chip to any cell of central field, which is already occupied, so he can move anywhere. Pay attention that this state of the game is unreachable.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9x9 grid divided into 9 smaller 3x3 fields (organized in a 3x3 grid)
2. Players take turns placing chips ('x' or 'o')
3. The game has special rules:
   - First move: can go to any cell
   - Subsequent moves: if the last move was in position (xl, yl) of some small field, the next move must be in the small field at position (xl, yl)
   - If there are no free cells in that required field, the player can move to any empty cell
4. We need to find all possible next moves for the current player

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 9 lines of the 9x9 grid
- The grid is divided by spaces and empty lines, so we need to parse it properly
- Read the coordinates (x, y) where the last move was made

### Step 2: Determine the small field of the last move
- The last move was at position (x, y) in the 9x9 grid
- Convert to 0-indexed: (x-1, y-1)
- Find which 3x3 small field this belongs to:
  - Row group: (x-1) // 3
  - Column group: (y-1) // 3
- This gives us the coordinates (r, c) of the small field where the last move occurred

### Step 3: Determine valid moves
- If the required small field has at least one empty cell (i.e., contains '.'), then the current player must move to one of the cells in that small field
- If the required small field has no empty cells, then the current player can move to any empty cell in any field

### Step 4: Generate output
- Create a copy of the original grid
- For each cell that is empty and valid according to the rules, mark it with '!'
- Output the grid in the same format

## Implementation

```python
# Read the input
lines = []
for _ in range(9):
    line = input().strip()
    lines.append(line)

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed
x_idx = x - 1
y_idx = y - 1

# Determine which small field the last move was in
field_row = x_idx // 3
field_col = y_idx // 3

# Create a list of the 9 small fields (each 3x3)
fields = []
for i in range(3):
    row = []
    for j in range(3):
        field = []
        for r in range(3):
            row_data = lines[i*3 + r]
            field.append(row_data[j*3:(j+1)*3])
        fields.append(field)

# Determine which cells are valid for the current player to move to
# The current player must move to a cell in the same small field as the last move
# unless that field has no empty cells

# Check if the field with (field_row, field_col) has any empty cells
has_empty_in_target_field = False
target_field = fields[field_row][field_col]
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        if target_field[i][j] == '.':
            has_empty_in_target_field = True
            break
    if has_empty_in_target_field:
        break

# Create result grid
result_lines = []
for i in range(9):
    row = ""
    for j in range(9):
        # Determine which small field this cell belongs to
        field_r = i // 3
        field_c = j // 3
        
        # Check if this cell is empty in the original grid
        if lines[i][j] == '.':
            # Check if this is a valid move
            if field_r == field_row and field_c == field_col:
                # This cell is in the target field - it's valid if the target field has empty cells
                if has_empty_in_target_field:
                    row += '!'
                else:
                    # Target field has no empty cells, so any empty cell is valid
                    row += '!'
            else:
                # This cell is not in the target field - only valid if target field has no empty cells
                if not has_empty_in_target_field:
                    row += '!'
                else:
                    row += '.'
        else:
            # Cell is already occupied
            row += lines[i][j]
    result_lines.append(row)

# Output the result
for line in result_lines:
    print(line)
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9×9 tic-tac-toe board divided into 9 smaller 3×3 fields
2. The game has specific rules for moves:
   - First move: player can put chip in any cell
   - Subsequent moves: if the last move was in cell (xl, yl) of some small field, the next move must be in the small field at position (xl, yl)
   - If there are no free cells in the required field, the player can move to any empty cell

3. Given the current state and the last move coordinates, we need to find all valid next moves.

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 11 lines of the 9×9 grid (divided by spaces and empty lines)
- Extract the 9 small 3×3 fields
- Parse the last move coordinates (x, y) - they're 1-indexed

### Step 2: Determine the rules for next moves
- The last move was at position (x, y) in the 9×9 grid
- Find which small field this position belongs to
- If that small field has no empty cells, the current player can move anywhere
- Otherwise, the current player can only move in that specific small field

### Step 3: Apply the rules
- For each cell in the 9×9 grid:
  - If the cell is empty (".") and it's in the restricted field, mark it with "!"
  - If the cell is empty and there's no restriction (i.e., the restricted field is full), mark it with "!"
  - Otherwise, keep the original character

### Step 4: Format the output
- Print the grid in the same format as input

## Implementation

```python
# Read the input
lines = []
for _ in range(11):
    lines.append(input().strip())

# Parse the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed coordinates
x_idx = x - 1
y_idx = y - 1

# The 9 small fields are structured as:
# Field 0: rows 0-2, cols 0-2
# Field 1: rows 0-2, cols 3-5
# Field 2: rows 0-2, cols 6-8
# Field 3: rows 3-5, cols 0-2
# Field 4: rows 3-5, cols 3-5
# Field 5: rows 3-5, cols 6-8
# Field 6: rows 6-8, cols 0-2
# Field 7: rows 6-8, cols 3-5
# Field 8: rows 6-8, cols 6-8

# Find which small field the last move belongs to
field_row = x_idx // 3
field_col = y_idx // 3

# Determine the restricted field
restricted_field = field_row * 3 + field_col

# Check if the restricted field has any empty cells
restricted_has_empty = False
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row = field_row * 3 + i
        col = field_col * 3 + j
        if lines[row][col * 3 + j] == '.':
            restricted_has_empty = True
            break
    if restricted_has_empty:
        break

# Create result grid
result_lines = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        cell = lines[i][j]
        if cell == '.':
            # Check if this cell is in the restricted field
            current_field_row = i // 3
            current_field_col = j // 3
            current_field = current_field_row * 3 + current_field_col
            
            # If it's in the restricted field and that field has empty cells, mark with "!"
            # If it's in the restricted field and that field has no empty cells, mark with "!" (can move anywhere)
            # If it's not in the restricted field, mark with "!" (can move anywhere when restricted field is full)
            if current_field == restricted_field:
                # If the restricted field has empty cells, only allow moves in that field
                if restricted_has_empty:
                    row.append('!')
                else:
                    row.append('!')
            else:
                # If the restricted field has no empty cells, can move anywhere
                row.append('!')
        else:
            row.append(cell)
    result_lines.append(''.join(row))

# Print the result with proper formatting
for i in range(9):
    if i < 2 or i >= 6:
        print(' '.join(result_lines[i].split()))
    else:
        print(' '.join(result_lines[i].split()))
```

Wait, I need to fix the printing format. The input has spaces between fields. Let me correct this:

View Extracted Code 
# Read the input
lines = []
for _ in range(11):
    lines.append(input().strip())

# Parse the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed coordinates
x_idx = x - 1
y_idx = y - 1

# Find which small field the last move belongs to
field_row = x_idx // 3
field_col = y_idx // 3

# Determine the restricted field
restricted_field = field_row * 3 + field_col

# Check if the restricted field has any empty cells
restricted_has_empty = False
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row = field_row * 3 + i
        col = field_col * 3 + j
        if lines[row][col * 3 + j] == '.':
            restricted_has_empty = True
            break
    if restricted_has_empty:
        break

# Create result grid
result_lines = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        cell = lines[i][j]
        if cell == '.':
            # Check if this cell is in the restricted field
            current_field_row = i // 3
            current_field_col = j // 3
            current_field = current_field_row * 3 + current_field_col
            
            # If it's in the restricted field
            if current_field == restricted_field:
                # If the restricted field has empty cells, allow this move
                row.append('!')
            else:
                # If the restricted field has no empty cells, allow this move
                row.append('!')
        else:
            row.append(cell)
    result_lines.append(''.join(row))

# Print the result in the same format as input
for i in range(9):
    if i < 2 or i >= 6:
        print(' '.join(result_lines[i].split()))
    else:
        print(' '.join(result_lines[i].split()))

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Two bears are playing tic-tac-toe via mail. It's boring for them to play usual tic-tac-toe game, so they are a playing modified version of this game. Here are its rules.

The game is played on the following field.

<image>

Players are making moves by turns. At first move a player can put his chip in any cell of any small field. For following moves, there are some restrictions: if during last move the opposite player put his chip to cell with coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move should be done in one of the cells of the small field with coordinates (xl, yl). For example, if in the first move a player puts his chip to lower left cell of central field, then the second player on his next move should put his chip into some cell of lower left field (pay attention to the first test case). If there are no free cells in the required field, the player can put his chip to any empty cell on any field.

You are given current state of the game and coordinates of cell in which the last move was done. You should find all cells in which the current player can put his chip.

A hare works as a postman in the forest, he likes to foul bears. Sometimes he changes the game field a bit, so the current state of the game could be unreachable. However, after his changes the cell where the last move was done is not empty. You don't need to find if the state is unreachable or not, just output possible next moves according to the rules.

Input

First 11 lines contains descriptions of table with 9 rows and 9 columns which are divided into 9 small fields by spaces and empty lines. Each small field is described by 9 characters without spaces and empty lines. character "x" (ASCII-code 120) means that the cell is occupied with chip of the first player, character "o" (ASCII-code 111) denotes a field occupied with chip of the second player, character "." (ASCII-code 46) describes empty cell.

The line after the table contains two integers x and y (1 ≤ x, y ≤ 9). They describe coordinates of the cell in table where the last move was done. Rows in the table are numbered from up to down and columns are numbered from left to right.

It's guaranteed that cell where the last move was done is filled with "x" or "o". Also, it's guaranteed that there is at least one empty cell. It's not guaranteed that current state of game is reachable.

Output

Output the field in same format with characters "!" (ASCII-code 33) on positions where the current player can put his chip. All other cells should not be modified.

Examples

In the second test case the last move was done to upper left cell of lower central field, however all cells in upper left field are occupied, so the second player can put his chip to any empty cell.

In the third test case the last move was done to central cell of central field, so current player can put his chip to any cell of central field, which is already occupied, so he can move anywhere. Pay attention that this state of the game is unreachable.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9×9 tic-tac-toe grid divided into 9 smaller 3×3 fields
2. The game has a special rule: after a move to cell (x, y) in some small field, the next move must be in the small field at position (x, y)
3. We're given the current state and the coordinates of the last move
4. We need to find all possible next moves for the current player

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the Input
- Read 11 lines of input (9 lines of fields separated by spaces/empty lines)
- Parse the 9×9 grid into 9 small 3×3 fields
- Extract the last move coordinates (x, y)

### Step 2: Determine the Rules for Next Moves
- The last move was made at position (x, y) in some small field
- This means the next move must be in the small field at position (x, y)
- However, if that small field has no empty cells, the player can move anywhere

### Step 3: Find Valid Moves
- Identify which small field is determined by the last move coordinates
- For that small field, find all empty cells (marked with ".")
- If no empty cells exist in that field, output "!" for all empty cells in the entire grid

### Step 4: Format Output
- Replace empty cells (".") with "!" only in the allowed positions
- Keep other cells unchanged

## Implementation

```python
# Read the input
lines = []
for _ in range(11):
    line = input().strip()
    if line:
        lines.append(line)

# Parse the 9x9 grid into 9 small 3x3 fields
fields = []
current_field = []
row_idx = 0

# Process each line to extract the 3x3 fields
for i in range(11):
    if i % 4 == 0 and i != 0:  # New field block
        if current_field:
            fields.append(current_field)
            current_field = []
    
    if i < 11 and i % 4 != 0:  # This is a line of a field
        if current_field:
            current_field.append(lines[i])
        else:
            current_field = [lines[i]]

# Add the last field
if current_field:
    fields.append(current_field)

# Extract the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed coordinates
x_idx = x - 1
y_idx = y - 1

# Determine which small field the last move was in
# The small fields are arranged in a 3x3 grid
# Small field at position (i, j) covers positions (3*i+1, 3*j+1) to (3*i+3, 3*j+3)
field_x = x_idx // 3
field_y = y_idx // 3

# Find all possible next moves
# The next move must be in the field at (field_x, field_y)
# But if that field has no empty cells, the player can move anywhere

# Check if the field at (field_x, field_y) has any empty cells
has_empty_in_target_field = False
target_field_cells = []

for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row = field_x * 3 + i
        col = field_y * 3 + j
        if row < 9 and col < 9:
            cell = fields[field_x][field_y][row * 3 + j] if fields[field_x] and fields[field_x][field_y] else '.'
            if cell == '.':
                has_empty_in_target_field = True
                target_field_cells.append((row, col))

# If there are empty cells in the target field, only those are valid moves
# Otherwise, all empty cells are valid (can move anywhere)
valid_moves = []

if has_empty_in_target_field:
    # Only the empty cells in the target field are valid
    for i in range(3):
        for j in range(3):
            row = field_x * 3 + i
            col = field_y * 3 + j
            if row < 9 and col < 9:
                cell = fields[field_x][field_y][row * 3 + j] if fields[field_x] and fields[field_x][field_y] else '.'
                if cell == '.':
                    valid_moves.append((row, col))
else:
    # Can move to any empty cell
    valid_moves = []
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if i < 9 and j < 9:
                cell = lines[i][j] if i < 9 and j < 9 and len(lines[i]) > j else '.'
                if cell == '.':
                    valid_moves.append((i, j))

# Now we need to rebuild the output with "!" for valid moves
output_lines = []

for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        cell = lines[i][j] if i < 9 and j < 9 and len(lines[i]) > j else '.'
        if (i, j) in valid_moves:
            row.append('!')
        else:
            row.append(cell)
    output_lines.append(' '.join(row))

# Print the result
for line in output_lines:
    print(line)

```python
# Read the 9 lines of the grid (with spaces between 3x3 fields)
grid_lines = []
for _ in range(9):
    grid_lines.append(input().strip())

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed
x_idx = x - 1
y_idx = y - 1

# Determine which 3x3 field the last move was in
field_x = x_idx // 3
field_y = y_idx // 3

# Parse the 9x9 grid from the input
grid = [['.' for _ in range(9)] for _ in range(9)]

# Process each line
for i in range(9):
    parts = grid_lines[i].split()
    for j in range(3):
        if j < len(parts):
            field_chars = parts[j]
            for k in range(3):
                if k < len(field_chars):
                    grid[i][j * 3 + k] = field_chars[k]
                else:
                    grid[i][j * 3 + k] = '.'

# Find valid next moves
# The next move must be in field (field_x, field_y) if that field has empty cells
# If it has no empty cells, any empty cell is valid

target_field_has_empty = False
valid_moves = []

# Check if target field has empty cells
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row = field_x * 3 + i
        col = field_y * 3 + j
        if row < 9 and col < 9 and grid[row][col] == '.':
            target_field_has_empty = True
            valid_moves.append((row, col))

if not target_field_has_empty:
    # Can move to any empty cell
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                valid_moves.append((i, j))

# Build output
result = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        if (i, j) in valid_moves:
            row.append('!')
        else:
            row.append(grid[i][j])
    result.append(' '.join(row))

# Print result
for line in result:
    print(line)

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9×9 grid divided into 9 smaller 3×3 fields
2. The game follows specific rules for moves:
   - First move: can go to any cell
   - Subsequent moves: if the last move was at position (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move must be in the small field at coordinates (xl, yl)
   - If no cells are available in that required field, the player can move to any empty cell

3. We're given the current state of the game and the coordinates of the last move
4. We need to output which cells the current player can move to, marking them with "!"

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 9 lines of the grid (some have spaces and empty lines)
- Extract the 9 small 3×3 fields
- Parse the last move coordinates (x, y) - these are 1-indexed

### Step 2: Determine which small field was used in the last move
- The coordinates (x, y) refer to the 9×9 grid
- Each small field is 3×3, so we can find which small field it belongs to:
  - field_row = (x-1) // 3
  - field_col = (y-1) // 3

### Step 3: Determine valid moves
- If the required small field has any empty cells (".") in it, the current player must move to one of those cells
- If the required small field has no empty cells, the current player can move to any empty cell

### Step 4: Output the result
- For each cell in the 9×9 grid:
  - If it's in the required small field AND it's empty, mark it with "!"
  - If it's not in the required small field AND it's empty, mark it with "!"
  - Otherwise keep the original character

Wait, let me reconsider the rule: "if during last move the opposite player put his chip to cell with coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move should be done in one of the cells of the small field with coordinates (xl, yl)"

This means:
- If the last move was at position (xl, yl) in field F, then the next move must be in field F
- But only if that field has empty cells
- If that field has no empty cells, then the player can move anywhere

### Step 5: Implementation

```python
# Read the 9 lines of the grid
grid_lines = []
for _ in range(9):
    line = input().strip()
    grid_lines.append(line)

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Determine which small field the last move was in
field_row = x // 3
field_col = y // 3

# Find the coordinates within the small field
field_x = x % 3
field_y = y % 3

# Create a 9x9 grid to store the result
result = [[''] * 9 for _ in range(9)]

# Process each cell in the 9x9 grid
for i in range(9):
    for j in range(9):
        # Get the original character at this position
        original_char = grid_lines[i][j]
        
        # Determine which small field this cell belongs to
        small_field_i = i // 3
        small_field_j = j // 3
        
        # Check if we're in the required field (same field as last move)
        if small_field_i == field_row and small_field_j == field_col:
            # We're in the required field - we can place here if it's empty
            if original_char == '.':
                result[i][j] = '!'
            else:
                result[i][j] = original_char
        else:
            # We're not in the required field - we can place here if it's empty
            if original_char == '.':
                result[i][j] = '!'
            else:
                result[i][j] = original_char

# Output the result in the original format
for i in range(9):
    # Join the 3 characters per field with spaces
    row = ""
    for j in range(9):
        row += result[i][j]
        if j % 3 == 2:  # After every 3 characters
            row += " "
    # Remove trailing space and print
    print(row.rstrip())
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Yet another round on DecoForces is coming! Grandpa Maks wanted to participate in it but someone has stolen his precious sofa! And how can one perform well with such a major loss?

Fortunately, the thief had left a note for Grandpa Maks. This note got Maks to the sofa storehouse. Still he had no idea which sofa belongs to him as they all looked the same!

The storehouse is represented as matrix n × m. Every sofa takes two neighbouring by some side cells. No cell is covered by more than one sofa. There can be empty cells.

Sofa A is standing to the left of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that x_{a} < x_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Sofa A is standing to the top of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that y_{a} < y_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Right and bottom conditions are declared the same way. 

Note that in all conditions A ≠ B. Also some sofa A can be both to the top of another sofa B and to the bottom of it. The same is for left and right conditions.

The note also stated that there are cnt_{l} sofas to the left of Grandpa Maks's sofa, cnt_{r} — to the right, cnt_{t} — to the top and cnt_{b} — to the bottom.

Grandpa Maks asks you to help him to identify his sofa. It is guaranteed that there is no more than one sofa of given conditions.

Output the number of Grandpa Maks's sofa. If there is no such sofa that all the conditions are met for it then output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer number d (1 ≤ d ≤ 10^5) — the number of sofas in the storehouse.

The second line contains two integer numbers n, m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^5) — the size of the storehouse.

Next d lines contains four integer numbers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 (1 ≤ x_1, x_2 ≤ n, 1 ≤ y_1, y_2 ≤ m) — coordinates of the i-th sofa. It is guaranteed that cells (x_1, y_1) and (x_2, y_2) have common side, (x_1, y_1)  ≠  (x_2, y_2) and no cell is covered by more than one sofa.

The last line contains four integer numbers cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} (0 ≤ cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} ≤ d - 1).


-----Output-----

Print the number of the sofa for which all the conditions are met. Sofas are numbered 1 through d as given in input. If there is no such sofa then print -1.


-----Examples-----
Input
2
3 2
3 1 3 2
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 1

Output
1

Input
3
10 10
1 2 1 1
5 5 6 5
6 4 5 4
2 1 2 0

Output
2

Input
2
2 2
2 1 1 1
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 0

Output
-1



-----Note-----

Let's consider the second example.   The first sofa has 0 to its left, 2 sofas to its right ((1, 1) is to the left of both (5, 5) and (5, 4)), 0 to its top and 2 to its bottom (both 2nd and 3rd sofas are below).  The second sofa has cnt_{l} = 2, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 2 and cnt_{b} = 0.  The third sofa has cnt_{l} = 2, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 1 and cnt_{b} = 1. 

So the second one corresponds to the given conditions.

In the third example   The first sofa has cnt_{l} = 1, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 0 and cnt_{b} = 1.  The second sofa has cnt_{l} = 1, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 1 and cnt_{b} = 0. 

And there is no sofa with the set (1, 0, 0, 0) so the answer is -1.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a storehouse represented as an n×m matrix
2. Each sofa occupies two adjacent cells (sharing a side)
3. We need to find which sofa has exactly `cnt_l` sofas to its left, `cnt_r` to its right, `cnt_t` to its top, and `cnt_b` to its bottom
4. "To the left" means there exists a cell in the sofa with smaller x-coordinate than a cell in another sofa
5. Similarly for right, top, and bottom

## Key Insights

1. For a sofa to have `cnt_l` sofas to its left, there must be exactly `cnt_l` sofas where at least one of their cells has a smaller x-coordinate than any cell in our sofa
2. Similarly for other directions
3. We need to check each sofa against the given constraints

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read number of sofas, matrix dimensions
- Read coordinates for each sofa
- Read the target counts (cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b)

### Step 2: For each sofa, calculate how many other sofas are to its left, right, top, bottom
- For "left": count sofas where min_x < current_sofa's min_x OR min_x < current_sofa's max_x (but we need to be careful about the actual cell positions)
- Actually, we need to check: a sofa A is to the left of sofa B if there's a cell in A with smaller x-coordinate than a cell in B

### Step 3: Check if the calculated counts match the target
- For sofa i, we need to count how many other sofas j have at least one cell with x < some cell in sofa i
- Similarly for other directions

### Step 4: Return the sofa number or -1 if none matches

Let me implement this:

```python
def solve():
    d = int(input())
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    sofas = []
    for i in range(d):
        x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
        sofas.append((x1, y1, x2, y2))
    
    cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # For each sofa, calculate how many other sofas are to its left, right, top, bottom
    for sofa_idx in range(d):
        x1, y1, x2, y2 = sofas[sofa_idx]
        
        # Find all x-coordinates covered by this sofa
        x_coords = [x1, x2]
        y_coords = [y1, y2]
        
        # Count how many other sofas are to the left of this sofa
        left_count = 0
        right_count = 0
        top_count = 0
        bottom_count = 0
        
        for other_idx in range(d):
            if other_idx == sofa_idx:
                continue
                
            x1_o, y1_o, x2_o, y2_o = sofas[other_idx]
            x_coords_o = [x1_o, x2_o]
            y_coords_o = [y1_o, y2_o]
            
            # Check if other sofa is to the left of current sofa
            # Other sofa is to the left if there's a cell in other sofa with x < min(x in current sofa)
            is_left = False
            for x_o in x_coords_o:
                if x_o < min(x_coords):
                    is_left = True
                    break
            
            # Check if other sofa is to the right of current sofa  
            # Other sofa is to the right if there's a cell in other sofa with x > max(x in current sofa)
            is_right = False
            for x_o in x_coords_o:
                if x_o > max(x_coords):
                    is_right = True
                    break
            
            # Check if other sofa is to the top of current sofa
            # Other sofa is to the top if there's a cell in other sofa with y < min(y in current sofa)
            is_top = False
            for y_o in y_coords_o:
                if y_o < min(y_coords):
                    is_top = True
                    break
            
            # Check if other sofa is to the bottom of current sofa
            # Other sofa is to the bottom if there's a cell in other sofa with y > max(y in current sofa)
            is_bottom = False
            for y_o in y_coords_o:
                if y_o > max(y_coords):
                    is_bottom = True
                    break
            
            if is_left:
                left_count += 1
            if is_right:
                right_count += 1
            if is_top:
                top_count += 1
            if is_bottom:
                bottom_count += 1
        
        # Check if this sofa matches the target counts
        if left_count == cnt_l and right_count == cnt_r and top_count == cnt_t and bottom_count == cnt_b:
            print(sofa_idx + 1)
            return
    
    print(-1)

solve()
```

Wait, I need to be more careful about the definitions. Let me reconsider:

According to the problem:
- Sofa A is to the left of sofa B if there exist cells a in A and b in B such that x_a < x_b
- This means the sofa with smaller x-coordinate in at least one cell is to the left

But when we're calculating for a sofa, we're counting how many other sofas are to its left, right, top, bottom.

Let me fix the logic:

```python
d = int(input())
n, m = map(int, input().split())

sofas = []
for i in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    sofas.append((x1, y1, x2, y2))

cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())

# For each sofa, calculate how many other sofas are to its left, right, top, bottom
for sofa_idx in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = sofas[sofa_idx]
    
    # Current sofa's coordinates
    current_x_min = min(x1, x2)
    current_x_max = max(x1, x2)
    current_y_min = min(y1, y2)
    current_y_max = max(y1, y2)
    
    left_count = 0
    right_count = 0
    top_count = 0
    bottom_count = 0
    
    for other_idx in range(d):
        if other_idx == sofa_idx:
            continue
            
        x1_o, y1_o, x2_o, y2_o = sofas[other_idx]
        other_x_min = min(x1_o, x2_o)
        other_x_max = max(x1_o, x2_o)
        other_y_min = min(y1_o, y2_o)
        other_y_max = max(y1_o, y2_o)
        
        # Check if other sofa is to the left of current sofa
        # Other sofa is to the left of current if there's a cell in other with x < some cell in current
        # This means: if other_x_max < current_x_min, then other sofa is completely to the left
        # Or if there's any cell in other with x < current_x_max and x >= current_x_min? No.
        
        # Actually: other sofa is to the left of current if there exists a cell in other with x < some cell in current
        # This happens if other_x_max < current_x_min (no overlap in x, other is completely left)
        # But actually, it's simpler: if min_x_in_other < max_x_in_current, then there's a cell in other with x < some cell in current?
        
        # Let me think: other sofa is to the left of current if there exists a cell in other with x < some cell in current
        # This is equivalent to: other_x_max < current_x_min OR other_x_min < current_x_max
        # Actually, if other_x_max < current_x_min, then all cells in other have x < all cells in current, so definitely to the left
        # If other_x_min < current_x_max, then there's some cell in other with x < some cell in current (specifically, any cell in other with x < current_x_max)
        
        # Actually, the condition for other sofa to be to the left of current is: 
        # there exists a cell in other and a cell in current such that x_other < x_current
        # This is true if other_x_max < current_x_min (other completely left) OR 
        # other_x_min < current_x_max (other overlaps or is partially right of current)
        
        # But actually, if other_x_min < current_x_max, then there's a cell in other with x_other >= other_x_min and x_other < current_x_max, 
        # so if we pick that cell and any cell in current with x >= x_other, we have x_other < x_current.
        
        # So: other sofa is to the left of current if other_x_min < current_x_max
        
        # Actually, no: if other_x_min = 1, current_x_max = 3, and other has cells at (1,1) and (2,2), current has cells at (3,1) and (3,2)
        # Then we have x_other=1 < x_current=3, so yes.
        
        # But if other_x_max = 2, current_x_min = 3, then other is completely to the left of current.
        # In this case other_x_min = 1 < current_x_max = 3, so it would be counted, which is correct.
        
        # So actually: other sofa is to the left of current if other_x_min < current_x_max
        
        # Similarly, other sofa is to the right of current if other_x_max > current_x_min
        
        # For y: other sofa is to the top of current if other_y_min < current_y_max
        # other sofa is to the bottom of current if other_y_max > current_y_min
        
        # But wait: "sofa A is to the left of sofa B" means A has a cell with smaller x than B has a cell
        # So for other sofa to be to the left of current sofa: there exists cell in other (with x_o) and cell in current (with x_c) such that x_o < x_c
        # This happens when other_x_min < current_x_max
        
        # Similarly for others:
        # Right: other_x_max > current_x_min
        # Top: other_y_min < current_y_max
        # Bottom: other_y_max > current_y_min
        
        # Actually, let me verify with the example:
        # First sofa: (3,1), (3,2) - x_min=3, x_max=3, y_min=1, y_max=2
        # Second sofa: (1,2), (2,2) - x_min=1, x_max=2, y_min=2, y_max=2
        # Is second to the left of first? Yes, because x_o=1 < x_c=3
        # Using our condition: other_x_min=1 < current_x_max=3 → true
        
        # Is second to the right of first? No, because x_o=2 < x_c=3, so no x_o > x_c
        # Using our condition: other_x_max=2 > current_x_min=3 → false
        
        # So our conditions seem correct.
        
        # So: other sofa is to the left of current if other_x_min < current_x_max
        # other sofa is to the right of current if other_x_max > current_x_min
        # other sofa is to the top of current if other_y_min < current_y_max  
        # other sofa is to the bottom of current if other_y_max > current_y_min
        
        is_left = (other_x_min < current_x_max)
        is_right = (other_x_max > current_x_min)
        is_top = (other_y_min < current_y_max)
        is_bottom = (other_y_max > current_y_min)
        
        if is_left:
            left_count += 1
        if is_right:
            right_count += 1
        if is_top:
            top_count += 1
        if is_bottom:
            bottom_count += 1
    
    # Check if this sofa matches the target counts
    if left_count == cnt_l and right_count == cnt_r and top_count == cnt_t and bottom_count == cnt_b:
        print(sofa_idx + 1)
        exit()

print(-1)
```

        # But wait: "sofa A is to the left of sofa B" means A has a cell with smaller x than B has a cell
        # So for other sofa to be to the left of current sofa: there exists cell in other (with x_o) and cell in current (with x_c) such that x_o < x_c
        # This happens when other_x_min < current_x_max
        
        # Similarly for others:
        # Right: other_x_max > current_x_min
        # Top: other_y_min < current_y_max
        # Bottom: other_y_max > current_y_min
        
        # Actually, let me verify with the example:
        # First sofa: (3,1), (3,2) - x_min=3, x_max=3, y_min=1, y_max=2
        # Second sofa: (1,2), (2,2) - x_min=1, x_max=2, y_min=2, y_max=2
        # Is second to the left of first? Yes, because x_o=1 < x_c=3
        # Using our condition: other_x_min=1 < current_x_max=3 → true
        
        # Is second to the right of first? No, because x_o=2 < x_c=3, so no x_o > x_c
        # Using our condition: other_x_max=2 > current_x_min=3 → false
        
        # So our conditions seem correct.
        
        # So: other sofa is to the left of current if other_x_min < current_x_max
        # other sofa is to the right of current if other_x_max > current_x_min
        # other sofa is to the top of current if other_y_min < current_y_max  
        # other sofa is to the bottom of current if other_y_max > current_y_min
        
        is_left = (other_x_min < current_x_max)
        is_right = (other_x_max > current_x_min)
        is_top = (other_y_min < current_y_max)
        is_bottom = (other_y_max > current_y_min)
        
        if is_left:
            left_count += 1
        if is_right:
            right_count += 1
        if is_top:
            top_count += 1
        if is_bottom:
            bottom_count += 1
    
    # Check if this sofa matches the target counts
    if left_count == cnt_l and right_count == cnt_r and top_count == cnt_t and bottom_count == cnt_b:
        print(sofa_idx + 1)
        exit()

print(-1)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Yet another round on DecoForces is coming! Grandpa Maks wanted to participate in it but someone has stolen his precious sofa! And how can one perform well with such a major loss?

Fortunately, the thief had left a note for Grandpa Maks. This note got Maks to the sofa storehouse. Still he had no idea which sofa belongs to him as they all looked the same!

The storehouse is represented as matrix n × m. Every sofa takes two neighbouring by some side cells. No cell is covered by more than one sofa. There can be empty cells.

Sofa A is standing to the left of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that x_{a} < x_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Sofa A is standing to the top of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that y_{a} < y_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Right and bottom conditions are declared the same way. 

Note that in all conditions A ≠ B. Also some sofa A can be both to the top of another sofa B and to the bottom of it. The same is for left and right conditions.

The note also stated that there are cnt_{l} sofas to the left of Grandpa Maks's sofa, cnt_{r} — to the right, cnt_{t} — to the top and cnt_{b} — to the bottom.

Grandpa Maks asks you to help him to identify his sofa. It is guaranteed that there is no more than one sofa of given conditions.

Output the number of Grandpa Maks's sofa. If there is no such sofa that all the conditions are met for it then output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer number d (1 ≤ d ≤ 10^5) — the number of sofas in the storehouse.

The second line contains two integer numbers n, m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^5) — the size of the storehouse.

Next d lines contains four integer numbers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 (1 ≤ x_1, x_2 ≤ n, 1 ≤ y_1, y_2 ≤ m) — coordinates of the i-th sofa. It is guaranteed that cells (x_1, y_1) and (x_2, y_2) have common side, (x_1, y_1)  ≠  (x_2, y_2) and no cell is covered by more than one sofa.

The last line contains four integer numbers cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} (0 ≤ cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} ≤ d - 1).


-----Output-----

Print the number of the sofa for which all the conditions are met. Sofas are numbered 1 through d as given in input. If there is no such sofa then print -1.


-----Examples-----
Input
2
3 2
3 1 3 2
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 1

Output
1

Input
3
10 10
1 2 1 1
5 5 6 5
6 4 5 4
2 1 2 0

Output
2

Input
2
2 2
2 1 1 1
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 0

Output
-1



-----Note-----

Let's consider the second example.   The first sofa has 0 to its left, 2 sofas to its right ((1, 1) is to the left of both (5, 5) and (5, 4)), 0 to its top and 2 to its bottom (both 2nd and 3rd sofas are below).  The second sofa has cnt_{l} = 2, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 2 and cnt_{b} = 0.  The third sofa has cnt_{l} = 2, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 1 and cnt_{b} = 1. 

So the second one corresponds to the given conditions.

In the third example   The first sofa has cnt_{l} = 1, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 0 and cnt_{b} = 1.  The second sofa has cnt_{l} = 1, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 1 and cnt_{b} = 0. 

And there is no sofa with the set (1, 0, 0, 0) so the answer is -1.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a storehouse represented as an n×m matrix
2. Each sofa occupies two neighboring cells (sharing a side)
3. For each sofa, we need to count how many other sofas are:
   - To the left: exists a cell in the left sofa with x-coordinate < x-coordinate of a cell in the right sofa
   - To the right: exists a cell in the right sofa with x-coordinate > x-coordinate of a cell in the left sofa
   - To the top: exists a cell in the top sofa with y-coordinate < y-coordinate of a cell in the bottom sofa
   - To the bottom: exists a cell in the bottom sofa with y-coordinate > y-coordinate of a cell in the top sofa

4. We're given cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b for Grandpa Maks's sofa
5. We need to find which sofa (numbered 1 to d) has exactly these counts

## Key Insights

1. For two sofas A and B, A is to the left of B if there exists a cell in A with x < x of some cell in B
2. This means: if A has any cell with x-coordinate less than any cell in B, then A is to the left of B
3. So for a sofa, the number of sofas to its left = count of sofas that have at least one cell with x < any cell of our sofa
4. Similarly for right, top, bottom

## Algorithm

1. For each sofa, calculate:
   - Count of sofas to its left (any cell in that sofa has x < any cell in other sofa)
   - Count of sofas to its right (any cell in that sofa has x > any cell in other sofa)
   - Count of sofas to its top (any cell in that sofa has y < any cell in other sofa)
   - Count of sofas to its bottom (any cell in that sofa has y > any cell in other sofa)

2. Compare these counts with the given cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b

3. Return the sofa number if it matches, otherwise -1

## Implementation Strategy

For each sofa i, to find sofas to its left:
- For each other sofa j, check if max(x_coordinates of sofa i) > min(x_coordinates of sofa j)
- If yes, then sofa j is to the left of sofa i

Wait, let me think again: "sofa A is to the left of sofa B" means there exists a cell a in A such that x_a < x_b for some b in B.

So for sofa A to be to the left of sofa B: there exists a cell in A with x-coordinate < some cell in B.

For a fixed sofa i, the number of sofas j such that sofa i is to the left of sofa j:
- This happens when min(x of sofa i) < some x of sofa j
- This means min(x of sofa i) < max(x of sofa j) (because if min(x_i) < max(x_j), then some x in i < some x in j)

Actually, let me re-read: "sofa A is to the left of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that x_a < x_b, a is covered by A and b is covered by B"

So A is to the left of B if min_x(A) < max_x(B)

Therefore:
- A is to the left of B iff min_x(A) < max_x(B)
- A is to the right of B iff max_x(A) > min_x(B)
- A is to the top of B iff min_y(A) < max_y(B)
- A is to the bottom of B iff max_y(A) > min_y(B)

So for each sofa, to find how many sofas are to its left:
For sofa i, count of sofas j such that min_x(i) < max_x(j)

Wait, no: we want sofas that are to the left of sofa i, not sofa i to the left of others.

Let me clarify:
- We want: how many sofas are to the left of sofa i?
- This is: count of sofas j such that sofa j is to the left of sofa i
- So: count of j such that min_x(j) < max_x(i)

Similarly:
- How many sofas are to the right of sofa i? Count of j such that max_x(j) > min_x(i)
- How many sofas are to the top of sofa i? Count of j such that min_y(j) < max_y(i)
- How many sofas are to the bottom of sofa i? Count of j such that max_y(j) > min_y(i)

Actually, let me verify with the definition:
- Sofa A is to the left of sofa B if x_a < x_b for some a in A, b in B
- So for sofa j to be to the left of sofa i: min_x(j) < max_x(i)

Yes, that's correct.

```python
d = int(input())
n, m = map(int, input().split())

sofas = []
for _ in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    # Store min and max coordinates for the sofa
    min_x = min(x1, x2)
    max_x = max(x1, x2)
    min_y = min(y1, y2)
    max_y = max(y1, y2)
    sofas.append((min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y))

cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())

# For each sofa, calculate how many sofas are to its left, right, top, bottom
for i in range(d):
    min_x_i, max_x_i, min_y_i, max_y_i = sofas[i]
    
    # Count sofas to the left of sofa i
    cnt_left = 0
    for j in range(d):
        if i == j:
            continue
        min_x_j, max_x_j, min_y_j, max_y_j = sofas[j]
        if min_x_j < max_x_i:  # sofa j is to the left of sofa i
            cnt_left += 1
    
    # Count sofas to the right of sofa i
    cnt_right = 0
    for j in range(d):
        if i == j:
            continue
        min_x_j, max_x_j, min_y_j, max_y_j = sofas[j]
        if max_x_j > min_x_i:  # sofa j is to the right of sofa i
            cnt_right += 1
    
    # Count sofas to the top of sofa i
    cnt_top = 0
    for j in range(d):
        if i == j:
            continue
        min_x_j, max_x_j, min_y_j, max_y_j = sofas[j]
        if min_y_j < max_y_i:  # sofa j is to the top of sofa i
            cnt_top += 1
    
    # Count sofas to the bottom of sofa i
    cnt_bottom = 0
    for j in range(d):
        if i == j:
            continue
        min_x_j, max_x_j, min_y_j, max_y_j = sofas[j]
        if max_y_j > min_y_i:  # sofa j is to the bottom of sofa i
            cnt_bottom += 1
    
    # Check if this sofa matches the required conditions
    if cnt_left == cnt_l and cnt_right == cnt_r and cnt_top == cnt_t and cnt_bottom == cnt_b:
        print(i + 1)
        exit()

# If no sofa matches, output -1
print(-1)
```

The last line contains four integer numbers cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} (0 ≤ cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} ≤ d - 1).

Print the number of the sofa for which all the conditions are met. Sofas are numbered 1 through d as given in input. If there is no such sofa then print -1.
